ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4026	Mrs. Federal Minister! I would like to ask you: [[/PAKT/VHG/XX/M/M_00141/index.shtml -- 141/M]] What results has the bonus malus system brought to older unemployed people?
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4053	Here you go.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4070	Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to allow myself to answer your question a little more in-depth, even under the mention of figures. One year after the entry into force of the bonus-malus system, the conclusions on the labour market impact are still somewhat uncertain, but I think I can say that there are already some positive trends. We made an evaluation for the period from September 1996 to May 1997 and found that the number of insured persons to be paid less or no service provider's contribution to unemployment insurance had doubled from 4 168 in September 1996 to 8 351 in May 1997. Most of this was due to the age group of 50 to 55, namely 6 095 persons or 73 percent of all bonus cases. No service providers were paid for 2 256 persons, between 55 and 59 years of age. On the other hand, the increase in malus cases – this was only 108 people – is practically not a matter of weight. In my opinion, the increased increase in bonus cases is a sign that job creation significantly outweighs the reduction of older workers. It has therefore been achieved that objective which we have met in the decision-making process. As a result, this positive effect of the bonus malus system is also reflected in the labour market data. For example, the number of men 55 to 59 years of age employed in self-employed employment in May 1997 was 7 100 or 7.3 percent, and the number of women 50 to 54 years of age was almost 2 000 or 1.9 percent above the previous year’s figure. We were therefore able to increase the proportion of employees in these age groups, so that I believe that we can say that we were able to implement a very interesting and innovative regulation with the bonus malus system, even if, of course, in the overall dimension these magnitudes represent a certain marginal size. But I believe that the direction is right.

I think we can see that this offers a very innovative, very flexible and very comprehensive programme for older unemployed.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4812	Thank you. We have thus completed this complex of questions. Mrs Reitsamer asks the next question.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4830	Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! I would like to ask you: [[/PAKT/VHG/XX/M/M_00137/index.shtml -- 137/M]] Are you going to provide for exceptions to or limits on the income of small employees in the social security system?
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4857	Please, Mrs. Minister.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e4873	Ladies and gentlemen! My aim is to ensure that, as far as possible, all workers are covered by the social protection of pension insurance, health insurance and accident insurance. I would also like to point out that employers' incentives are to be eliminated by the reduction of full-time jobs and the simultaneous allocation of low-employment costs to competitors. The expert group, which I have used to draw up proposals for implementation of these objectives, also claimed that there are numerous cases of minor employment where social protection is already available, such as co-insurance, and where a contribution obligation would be qualified by the parties concerned as a threat to their minor benefits. I consider these objections to be problematic insofar as the distortion of competition on the employer's side would not be eliminated in the event of further differentiation, and I believe that the employer's contribution should not in any way lead to such differentiation. An option was discussed in the expert group in the contribution, with such a regulation, from my point of view, having advantages, but also disadvantages. In addition, the question was also discussed as to whether there should be a ceiling on the amount of the social security obligation to pay on a case-by-case basis a maximum of 1 000 s per month in order not to overload the administration in these cases. From my point of view, however, this proposal is not unproblematic either. I therefore intend to continue this very difficult discussion both at the technical level and at the political level in the summer weeks in order to achieve as broad a consensus as possible on the basis of the planned review procedure in order to achieve the objective, including all minor employees, involvement in social security protection and the elimination of competitive advantages. I hope that this solution will be accepted and carried along by a large number of those concerned, and I also hope that this will not create any additional administrative burdens.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e6420	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! A few weeks ago, the three parliamentary opposition parties remained distant from the Committee's deliberations, because we felt that the possibilities for parliamentary control in the practice of this House were so much reduced that the exercise of the control tasks, in particular the opposition, seems to be at risk, is no longer possible. The government parties have initially replied to this committee boycott with a lack of understanding. They criticised the distance from the Committee's deliberations, and they did not want to understand that this was not a protest which was started on the basis of a point of adjustment, but that it was really a call, an act of emergency and an act which was and is of great importance to parliamentarianism in Austria as a whole. In the meantime, the development seems to be right for the opposition parties. Neither during this legislative period nor during the two previous legislative periods, it has been possible to carry out a parliamentary investigation on any subject in the media, in the Austrian population, in need of information. The House has thus reached a very far distance from a previous parliamentary use. It was before that, at least in some very important matters of parliamentary use, that the majority of parliament also agreed to a parliamentary inquiry by the representatives of the government parties in centrally intelligible materials. This has been the case, in particular, in connection with large State investment, particularly in the arms sector. This has always been the case in relation to the protection of citizens and human rights, such as the protection against unlawful police attacks, telephone hearings and the like. And that was also the case in connection with large-scale construction projects, with the activity of large construction companies. Although it was never easy to achieve such a parliamentary investigation, several attempts were always necessary, but at some point there was the view of the majority of this House that such accusations should not remain in the room and that the House as a whole is guilty of informing the Austrian population. From this practice, the representatives of the government parties, not least in the light of the last three committees of inquiry. The arguments put forward have stated in the Tenor that these committees of inquiry on the topics of "Noricum", "Lucona", "Milchwirtschaft" had accepted the character of Tribunals and that persons who were initially heard as witnesses as information persons suddenly became accused by contradictory, incorrect statements in detail. It was complained that there were no procedural regulations for such committees of inquiry and therefore no protection for information subjects, for people who were consulted before these committees of inquiry. The reasons for not having such a procedural order were not in the opposition. There were debates at the beginning of the 1990s, and none of the three parliamentary opposition parties failed to reach the Austrian People's Party. Now it cannot and must not be that the lack of a parliamentary procedure for committee of inquiry leads to a refusal of control itself. It cannot be said that the reference to procedural standards is an important, the hardest and sharpest parliamentary control right at all becomes a dead law. The fact that there is an interest in information of the population, the media, the general public in incidents, such as the political interrelationships in connection with the Kurds, is probably not going to dispute anyone in this House. The fact that the Rules of Procedure have been discussed very seriously is that there is now a draft which has been put in place and on which this first reading takes place today, I regard as a clear success and progress, triggered by the boycott of the Committee, caused by a very factual and well-understood call by the opposition parties. The developments since then give us a right. All that has ever been done on documents from the government side, especially from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, cannot be put in place of parliamentary inquiry. All that has so far been presented by the government and oralally here in this House is passing by the central political question of why, in the face of a very likely, almost safe state terror in this matter, the Republic of Austria’s kneelfall before Iran. There have been murders, and it has not been all done to bring to light the backgrounds of these murders, of the middle men, of the organization, of the logistical braid, of the behind, of the light and of the complete investigation, but of the perpetrators. – More: They were escorted to the airport, they made their escape very easy. To this day, the question of why this happened has not been answered. Those answers, as they were to be heard from the government here in this House – it was embarrassing mistakes, sluttings, misunderstandings – are deeply unbelievable, or they have clearly proved to be wrong in the meantime. Only in this House can the question be resolved whether there was a case of state emergency at that time legitimately or at least in a debateable manner. I think there is hardly anyone – even in the opposition parties – who, in certain cases, would not justify the abandonment of the state and its supreme organs in the face of the terrorist act, but would probably understand in some way. Yes, there may be circumstances under which a surrender, a temptation may be the appropriate procedure to prevent even more suffering, even more bloodshed, and serious harm at least at first. It is clear that such a situation is always dangerous, alarming and very difficult for a rule of law. That's why it's about political enlightenment. This is not a question of criminal courts, it is not a question which can be resolved elsewhere by means of documents, by letter of the authorities. It is not about bureaucratic incentives in the security of the embassy or the like, it is about the central political question: Under what circumstances can and may the State, in cases of terrorism, not deal with the full hardness of the law? These circumstances need to be clarified. The mere fear of order loss or even the fear that perhaps any dubious business might come to light does not justify the kneefall. This central question: Was it the legitimate fear of caring about the lives of Austrians in Iran, was it the legitimate concern for further acts of terror, or was it the illegal fear that perhaps dubious business, perhaps arms business, could be revealed? It can only be clarified in the context of a parliamentary investigation. [[Congratulation with the Greens and the Abg. Dr. Schmidt.]] The discussions we have held in this regard in the Presidency are partly going in a completely wrong direction. We have discussed all the serious issues in the Presidency – with a negative outcome, of course – whether there should again be something similar to a rejection of the Majesty policy in relation to the head of state, in relation to the top representatives of the Republic of Austria. It may not be the right way for legitimate criticism or the pursuit of Enlightenment to be presented at once as illegal or semi-legitim, but is the only correct and correct answer: Of course, there should be and must be respect for the top organs of the Republic of Austria, but this respect can only be safeguarded and maintained in the long term if there is not the slightest doubt about the correctness and the integrity of the actions and motives of these supreme organs. Explain yourself here and today! It would bring back a lot of political respect to the public.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e6562	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! The debate on the Rules of Procedure is democratic, because the Rules of Procedure of a Parliament or of a country have a great deal to say about the democratic standards that a country has. I believe that, in the context of such a debate, there should be a number of questions, but they must also be answered. The first question is: What institutions and instruments does a State have to ensure its rule of law? The second question is: How are these designed? Can they also fulfil their purpose with this design? And the third question is: How are they handled in practice? Now, if I ask myself, Which bodies, which instruments does a state have to ensure the rule of law?, I am also involved in projects of this coalition government that we will be discussing in the next few days, such as noise attack and grid arrest. I think that it is also part of the democratic standards to consider together what instruments one can create, regardless of whether one wants to rely on or rely on these instruments or whether they are properly used. The first stage is the question: What are we making available as an instrument? . I am afraid that tomorrow in this House, if you want to debate and decide on this proposal here, we will have a wrong course. As far as the institutions and instruments we have to ensure the rule of law are concerned, we will hopefully all agree: At the heart of these institutions, Parliament, the Parliament, which is called to ensure the rule of law, is at the heart of the House with its instruments, to which I will go even more closely afterwards. However, there are also other institutions, such as the highest courts: Administrative Court, Constitutional Court, and Parliament's Control Instruments: Public prosecutorship, Court of Auditors, and the instruments of direct democracy. As far as the People's Procuratorate is concerned, we will also discuss a proposal here. The Public Prosecutorship is an essential instrument for control, but at the moment a path is being taken which is depriving more and more of this control instrument, through the outsourcing of various institutions, which, while having the majority of the federal government in their ownership structure, are therefore fully controlled by the Court of Auditors, but which are withdrawn from the Public Prosecutor's Office's control instrument. I therefore think it is important, if we are serious about the control instruments, that we start a follow-up procedure in this area. We Liberals have tabled a relevant request. But even though I am thinking of the highest courts, of the Constitutional Court, I believe that there is a serious need for action in this area. In almost all countries of the world there is the establishment of the so-called "dissising opinion", i.e. the divergent opinion. We have also discussed this issue with the coalition parties, and we agreed, Mr Khol, that this dissenting opinion of the Constitutional Court would be an essential contribution to our democratic standards. For it is part of the basic democratic standard that the decision of a Supreme Court is rationally understandable that the individual considerations can be interpreted. A minority opinion can therefore also help to make the perceptibility clearer in one direction or the other, in order to create transparency. How many times has it been that a minority opinion has developed into a majority opinion if it has been disclosed in its argument? You only need to look at developments in other countries! Only in Austria are we afraid to make a minority opinion clear, to present publicly, in order not only to allow anyone to think independently or to give the opportunity to perhaps include a different perspective in the considerations. I think it is essential to regulate the divergent opinion in order to bring our democratic standards to the minimum that we already have in other countries. For example, we have the instrument of mistrust. In Parliament, a majority of a member of the government can distrust. However, we are now in a situation where we insist that the Rules of Procedure do not provide for clear rules. It cannot be that there must be an item on the agenda, in the context of which a request for distrust can be made at all! It cannot be that one must wait until by chance there is an appropriate presentation for a member of the government to express distrust! In this respect too, we need a re-examination of the rules in the Rules of Procedure. We have a proper establishment, namely the Standing Subcommittee of the Court of Auditors, but if I experience how the rules are handled there, I am not satisfied with that. – I admit, I jump to my third question: How are the instruments handled in practice? – In itself, the establishment of this Standing Subcommittee of the Court of Auditors is a very important tool for political control. On the initiative of the Liberals, we have also successfully implemented this in the case of the contract regulations. And the result also shows that the correction was necessary. I do not mean that it is the natural right of a two-thirds majority of Parliament to develop the Constitution or to change it – no one speaks of it! – but it is a question of using the two-thirds majority only from the perspective of turning off the Constitutional Court as a control body over fundamental principles. ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e6802	The next speaker is Dr. Kostelka. He's got the floor.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e6823	Mr President. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! On the one hand, the four first readings, which are being discussed together here, address two concerns: on the one hand, the implementation of a rule of law, of a fair procedure even before committee of inquiry, I would like to add: precisely before committees of inquiry, because the rule of law must not stop at the gates of this House, on the other hand, the concern of the opposition, the establishment of investigation committees as a minority right. Ladies and gentlemen! Let me first turn to the question of the procedure and the rule of law in the case of committee of inquiry, which was put together with Mr Khol. In the years following 1920, in the whole of the second Republic, there was a very dry and narrow reference to the criminal procedure with regard to the examination committees, and there were hardly any problems with this very narrow standard of reference until 1985. To formulate it in detail: The discussions resulting from this lack of parliamentary procedural law were issues of effectiveness and, in particular, the interpretation of the extent to which this criminal procedure could really be implemented in a parliamentary procedure. But there has hardly been any differences about the clarity and the commonness of the procedure. This has also been expressed in the results of research committees. We have generally had joint reports in the first committees of inquiry, and if so, there have been minority reports to a small extent in the overall report. This has changed. This has changed very sustainably with the climate in this House. It has become more controversial, and as a result, research committees have become simple and simply more controversial. I do not notice this blamingly and critically, but only as someone who tries to evaluate the symptoms. But this also means, ladies and gentlemen, that the opposition has understood less than a part of the whole, but that the opposition has increasingly tried to implement and achieve previously defined objectives in committee of inquiry. If you have experienced research committees – and I have been able to do so in a number of functions – then you have increasingly been given the impression that some of the participants have been asked to receive predetermined judgments. And this, ladies and gentlemen, is a fundamental attitude which is simply and simply contrary to this procedure. To quote a member of this Committee of Inquiry, which I have experienced: "It was about putting on the golden shot." However, this is not with the position of an independent judge, and therefore the procedure we have applied so far is inconceivable with a self-understanding as we have increasingly found it. Ladies and gentlemen! No one less than the judge and later Federal President Dr. Kirchschlager found in this context as early as 1983: If, in committee of inquiry, Members stand as prosecutors and judges in a person, where is the principle of fairness, the equality of the rule of law? In this context, Professor Matscher, the Austrian representative of the European Human Rights Commission for decades, has stated that we have sometimes seen in committee of inquiry, as in the inquisition, that, in particular, with this amalgamation of judges and prosecutors, guarantees for an objective presentation of matters have been lost. That is why this right of procedure proposed by us, which we have implemented very consensually in five-party discussions. The aim was to establish the rule of law, the equality of arms and fairness, even though judges and prosecutors act in a person in these proceedings before committee of inquiry, and we also know only witnesses, but every witness can happen immediately to get to the position of the defendant, and he has no claim to defence under current law. I would like to add, with pleasure, that the discussions we have held as representatives of all five parties have been in a very constructive climate and have also made many common positions; the detailed arrangements are largely appropriate. But I also ask here at the beginning of the deliberations that we have to take on board, whether we already have the entire path to be taken, because, in some cases, a complete equality of judicial procedures with investigation committees will not be possible. In this context, I only point to the right of the defendant to reject the judge for partiality. This is excluded from an examination committee procedure from the outset. [[Abg. Wabl: This is a completely different thing! There is no judgment either!]] But in this context, for example, we have not yet established the right, which is absolutely natural in any court proceedings, that, for example, the defendant or the defendant in an indicted position may also ask the witnesses questions. We will still have to discuss the equality of fairness in the process in further discussions. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! In this context, however, I am concerned with another important point: In a truly fair procedure, there must be no pre-conception in the interplay of media and judicially acting member of the investigative committee. We are simply at the limits of procedural law. I am convinced that this will ultimately not be normalized, and that beyond this procedure we will also need a code of conduct which excludes such prejudgment by a judge before the conclusion of a case-law procedure, with the aid of the media. [[President Dr. Brauneder shall take over the chairmanship.]] Ladies and gentlemen! The procedure is before you; I can take three perspectives: The task of the investigation is to be made much clearer, the silence of office to Parliament – that is, a fundamental exception to the silence of office – and the lawyer will also ensure a higher level of fairness. The opposition parties have, in part before our request, made a request for the establishment of a committee of inquiry as a minority law, and in this context very, very harsh words have been made in the direction of establishing such a minority right, which is a fundamental requirement of every parliamentaryism. . I should like to point out to you that thirteen of the 15 states of the European Union cannot build on this foundation, and that is why such a globalisation is certainly not permitted. If you already use such formulas from my point of view, you should also be aware that, for example, in the Federal Republic of Germany, which you have repeatedly used as an example, Parliament has no instruments like the Court of Auditors, because it is judicially and independently organized there. In our view, orders to the Court of Auditors as Parliament's instrument are not permitted there at all. There is therefore no subcommittee of the Court of Auditors there in this sense and no parliamentary deliberations such as we in the Court of Auditors. I would also ask you to take into account that there is no other Parliament in which 2.7% of Members have the right to convene special meetings of the National Council, in which 2.7% of the legislative body have the right – as well as today – to submit applications to members of the Federal Government, but also requests with a warning period of three hours. We shall have to consider your request, as well as ours, and we will have to discuss it in detail, but I may ask you not to assume in the discussion that the law of the majority cannot be the law of democracy. I am open to these deliberations.

The residue of the unfinished files amounted to 4 107 cases. The President's forecast was that the year 1996 will end with a total of 14 000 unfinished procedures – that is the sum of the total residue – and the residue will increase by 4,000 cases each year if – according to the addition – nothing is done. Two points had to be taken into account when setting up the independent Bundesasylsenate, namely the urgent discharge of the Administrative Court and the guarantee of legal protection for asylum seekers in full. The Bundesasylsenat is to decide on complaints against decisions by the Federal Asylamt of the Ministry of the Interior as the top appeal authority in asylum matters. This is a court-like institution based on the model of the independent administrative offices. What does this mean for the members of this Senate? – Independence, integrity and appointment at an indefinite time, i.e. the withdrawal can only be effected by the withdrawal of the assembly. The purpose of this particular status is to ensure the free, impartial and objective decision-making of the Senate members, who decide as individual judges and not in chambers. The Bundesasylsenat is thus referred to the Administrative Court as the appeal authority. This is an important function, in addition to safeguarding the rule of law, of administrative policy. Another means of remission of the administrative court – which I quite agree with – is the granting of the right of refusal in those cases where an independent administrative Senate or the independent federalasylsenate has decided, thus, a preliminary instance. This means that a complaint can only reach the Administrative Court in exceptional cases. Consequently, the Administrative Court may in future reject the treatment of complaints if the decision does not contribute to clarifying a legal situation which is in principle a matter for the Administrative Court. I consider the amendments proposed today to be a very good means of administrative policy. I am convinced that these measures, which will be implemented accordingly, will bring the expected success in the near future. I would also like to stress that the creation of the Bundesasylsenat has not only been supported by the administrative authorities but also was welcomed by the representatives of the asylum seekers and human rights organisations. For it is obvious that completely overburdened complaints and for years unfinished procedures are also problematic in terms of legal protection and legal certainty. The Bundesasylsenat can therefore be seen as a measure which ensures access to the law, namely the fundamental right to appeal. Therefore, it is not rare to lack the awareness of costs, the proportionality, the cost of the administrative burden caused by a complaint, as measured by the proportionality of the request. 2 500 S could also be seen as a threshold before which each individual citizen has to examine whether his complaint could also have a chance of success, or whether his concern is important enough to deal with it at the highest level, because at least 40 percent of complaints to the Administrative Court are dismissed as unfounded and another 40 percent for formal reasons. ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e8471	Mr President. Mr. State Secretary! High house! I too and the Liberal Forum consider it of course an important step if the ban on equality in Article 7 of the Constitution is supplemented by an explicit ban on discrimination against people with disabilities, because – already mentioned here – discrimination of disabled people is still commonplace in everyday life. I do not want to adjure the personal commitment of the Secretary-General to smoke-callat, but it was a hard fight to move the ÖVP, in the final consequence, from its original attitude towards this then very clear and very positive step. I would like to follow the colleague's remarks that it is to a great extent thanks to the disabled associations that they have been able to prevent a further delay in the final consequence with great commitment. They have greatly contributed to the change of consciousness in this House. I have personally felt it valuable support to be able to share in their experiences and information. I would also like to thank you very much for this. Ladies and gentlemen! This constitutional provision is a first important step, because the constitutional anchorage creates at least an increased obligation to state reasons for discrimination. Unfortunately, I am sure that, because of our current legal situation, the disabled organisations will have a broad field of activity if it is necessary to continue to support those affected by it in the final consequence. I am thinking only of the laws recently adopted in this House in connection with integration. In the area of integration, secondary education is now subject to the rules in Austria that organisational reasons are sufficient to prevent the inclusion of a child into an integrative class in the secondary sector. This organisational justification will no longer be sufficient in future, as a constitutional court ruling in Germany already makes clear. In any event, this first important step must take many more steps, and I believe that the activities should start as far as possible today. First of all, it is important to search through all legal provisions at all levels – federal, state and local authorities – in order to show discrimination and then reorganise them accordingly. We should also make a quick effort to develop a law on equality, as it has been in the USA and the UK since 1990 and as is already being discussed in many other European countries. In any event, such a law on equality must ensure that all people, especially disabled people, have access to all educational institutions, that our schools are equipped with disabilities – we need a great deal of follow-up – and that there is no discriminatory provision for the inclusion of disabled children in the school system. If Mr Rauch-Kallat has spoken so beautifully about the fact that we are not allowed to play out mentally disabled people and people with disabilities, then I must say that she is in a state of tension with the Minister of Education and even with the decisions she has taken here in this House. In the area of school integration, we distinguish very well between mental, sensory and physical impairments. In particular, we fail to provide any support to the children with disabilities in secondary education, unless these children would also be marked with mental impairment in a special educational centre. Two very specific cases have become very up-to-date in recent times. I remember the girl Anja in Reutte, who is hearing impaired, who fails to provide further training and where the guardian's premeditation by the legislator – or in this case by the administration – goes even so far that the data protection law is violated and that, without talking to the girl, the school success of this girl is publicly represented in the media with the help of the National School Council. I also recall a current case involving a girl in Vienna who is forbidden to repeat a class. The Minister of Education also believes that a repetition of this class cannot ensure that the completion of the school will be positive. I really wonder if we can talk about equal treatment if repetitions – the number of repetitious people in Austria is 50 000 – are of course. We need changes in transport facilities, public buildings and equality in contracts. A law on equality would be the basis for the complaint of discrimination and the claim for damages. There would be a wide range of activities to be undertaken at the earliest possible time. Unfortunately, I no longer have the necessary time available, but I would like to conclude with the following sentence: It is a question of human dignity that we allow all disabled people a life of self-determination. Therefore, we should be more careful when selecting words when we use terms such as special facilities, protected workshops, care and so on. The task of policy should be to distance itself from the personal design of life of all people, and in particular from the premeditation of people with disabilities, and to guarantee them genuine equality through a clear legal position.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e9365	Mr President. Mr State Secretary! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) During this term of office, we have undertaken a very ambitious programme ranging from a UOG novelle that will be on the agenda in a few hours, through university law of study to service law reform. Today, it is about a profound transformation of the law of the service, and there is not a completely new but strongly changed right of service. This was mainly negotiated since early summer last year. Even though my previous speaker spoke of a flicking work, all the groups involved in it stand up to the present outcome of the negotiations. It shows that the social partners also work on a university level. I would like to deal less with the legislative text – it is explained in detail in the supplements – than with the few points in which we have allowed ourselves to deviate a little from the outcome of the negotiations. The problem with service law is that on the one hand, there is a result negotiated by service providers and service representatives and on the other hand the Parliament, which is the Members who also have to make an opinion on this and decide on the law. On the few points where deviations have occurred, the discussion with the affected groups has already been sought. It is about the new professors of the contract – this is a new type of university teachers in connection with the UOG 1993 – and the foundation professors, as they already exist worldwide at different universities and with whom we now want to gain first experience. I assume that Mr Lukesch will take a closer look at this issue. From the outcome of the negotiations, we are also in the withdrawal scheme, but also not without questioning the groups concerned for their different positions. Emeritation is now no longer possible at the request of the person concerned, but the faculty takes a decision accordingly. It is admittedly a question of assessment, in which there is a more favourable arrangement for those concerned, both for the faculty and for the professors to be emeritus. We believe that there should first be a decision by the faculty; of course, discussions will be held with those concerned beforehand. The scheme was also amended by the fact that, limited to two years, the teaching obligations of the assistants were extended by two hours, so that those institutions that would otherwise have had major problems could maintain and guarantee the teaching firm. Let me now turn to Mr Lucisch's motion for a resolution and to me, which contains the task of developing a new right of service within three years. There has already been a first letter of protest asking: Why do Members want to redesign something that we have negotiated? In the BDG, you can see the rules relevant to the university teachers on a long range of supplementary fees, which depend on the classification, namely overtime remuneration, flat-rate compensation for an extended schedule, Sunday and holiday allowances, periodic service allowance, willingness compensation, additional benefit allowance, remuneration, difficulty allowance, risk allowance, expenses compensation, loss compensation and so on – I want to save you from reading the full list for time. However, there are not only the BDG, but also the VBG, the pension law, the salary law and a number of other laws, regulations and decrees that come into play. (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Such a situation is most pleasing to the employee. Because the service provider has experts, we know, excellent experts who deal with and recognise the law of the service. The individual official, however, sees himself as a very inconspicuous plethora of rules. That is why I think it would be good for the universities to take a step forward in this regard. In conclusion, I should like to mention five points which my group believes should be reflected in the new service law. Firstly, it is about quality in teaching and research. It is the most important thing for our universities. Measures to promote quality concern continuing training, the promotion of "scientific young people" – as it is called – and the arrangements for reviewing quality. The latter takes place in connection with the UOG in the context of the evaluation, but has not yet found an entry into the service law. Secondly, women need to find better and fair conditions at universities. This is a crucial prerequisite for scientific careers. We know from the statistics that women are still disadvantaged for various reasons. We can counter this in the law of the service. The third is mobility: Regional mobility and interdisciplinary mobility between administrative activities, teaching and research, as well as in non-university institutions. It is about facilitating the change between the various areas and also the return to an earlier activity. Fourthly, it is about independence of scientific responsibility for what is being taught and researched, including ethical responsibility. Especially at a time when the research landscape is being redesigned and more order research is to be made possible, this is an important requirement. The scientist and the scientist must not be left alone in their ethical responsibility. The fifth is the balance between safety and risk. It is also important for science that orientation cannot take place alone in the direction of safety. Mobility opens up opportunities for scientific careers, but also carries risks. Finally, I would like to make a statement and express my thanks. Through the UOG 1993 and its implementation, the University Study Act, as well as the budget renovation, high demands were placed on the universities and on all those working there, from the rector to the housemaster and electrician. All of them have been strongly claimed in the course of this restructuring. It takes a lot of motivation to cope. That is why I would also like to say from this point of view that excellent work is being done at our universities. Thank you very much in the House! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e9865	Mr President. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In these times of time limit, I will try to keep my remarks very short, especially as we will have other opportunities to convince you of our arguments. It is not the time of voting today, but we have a first reading, and I would therefore like to explain very briefly the reasons which have led us to this request. [[Unrest in the hall.]] I understand that you are not very interested in instruments of direct democracy, as I even feel that democracy is not exactly a matter of heart to you. [[President Dr. Fischer gives the bell sign.]] It is only that this leaves a bad impression on those whose representatives you are here. - You're sitting there at the top of the gallery. [[Congratulations at the Liberal Forum.]] I do not want to exaggerate you as far as my speaking time is concerned, but only to say that, when there is talk of the liveliness of democracy, most of the instruments of direct democracy are used. A popular desire is certainly a very suitable instrument to express directly the will of the population. It is also a very old instrument: It was already contained in the Constitution of 1920, but with different conditions. Once again there were 10,000 signatures needed, then 30,000, then 10,000. The current state of affairs is that such a popular desire can introduce 10,000 citizens or 8 national council delegates or 4 parliamentarians each. I think that this is a completely unacceptable privilege for Members, both as regards members of the National Council and Members of Parliament. I believe that the instrument of popular desire should remain a public instrument. I think it is also curious that national councillors introduce a popular desire because it is ultimately the goal of a popular desire to lead the legislator to deal with a particular matter. I think that this detour to get certain signatures and support is for the admission of a weak self-understanding. If Parliamentarians believe that one should treat a particular matter, they have other possibilities at hand and do not need to articulate the path of popular desire. I believe, rather, that the danger of party-tactical supremacy, so that in the end the risk of abuse – the risk of inflation of popular desires and the like – is much greater, and that could ultimately lead to a dampening of this instrument, which would be bad. But I do not think it necessary, for example, to reduce the number of signatures. I say this after looking at how statements of support have been found in an opening procedure: Even one of the last popular desires, which was one of the weakest supported, namely the popular desire "per motorcycle", has received nearly 13,000 support signatures for the introduction. I therefore believe that there is no need for action to be taken under the requirement of 10,000 signatures. However, there is a need for action in the direction of abolishing the privilege of Members. I would like the instrument of popular desire to become a purely public instrument, that it should not be used by individual parties as a pre-electing machine, but that Parliament should deal with the public's will in the way they expect. You will have the opportunity to demonstrate, on the basis of the genetic engineering popular desire, how seriously you take popular desires. But delete the privilege of parliamentarians! - Thank you.

Every democratic constitution in modern states knows institutions, institutes, direct democracy and indirect democracy. These are different from one country to another, from one state to another: Here the direct elements have grown historically stronger, elsewhere not so strong. In Switzerland, for example, the institutions of direct democracy, i.e. plebiscitary democracy, are much more pronounced than in Austria, for example. The Institutes of Austrian Direct Democracy are essentially popular desire, referendum, popular survey and, of course, all elections. In turn, elections fix the representatives, who then have a certain time for the people to make decisions. In concrete terms today we are dealing with popular desire, quite specifically – I do not need to repeat it, Mrs Schmidt has already said – the question of the form in which we Members should be involved in the initiation process for popular desires. [[New congratulations to the ÖVP.]] In 1989, there was a parliamentary survey, one of several in which a speaker – I quote – said: I have shown that we are not even able to really monitor the 0.8-promille limit quite seriously and to ensure that it is not exceeded. I have also shown that a transition from 0.8 to 0.5 cannot bring as much as better surveillance. Ladies and gentlemen! That's our position, too, and the man who said that was the then Minister of Transport Dr. Streicher. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I am telling you this only because you should also admit that, like Minister Streicher at the time, we are also representing a legitimate transport policy position today, especially since nothing has changed in this position. It is true that only the Social Democratic Transport Ministers have changed, but not the positions and facts on this issue. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This position is represented by many experts. It has also been represented by many social-democratic transport politicians for many years, and rightly so, for 53 percent of the drivers who were alcoholically caught, according to Austrian studies, had a promille degree of between 1.5 and 2.5 permills, and the mean value of the decreased blood samples in recent years has remained constant at about 1.5 permills. Only every thousandth of alcoholized drivers, says the Curatorium for Traffic Safety, are also discovered by the executive. Ladies and gentlemen! That is the real reason! This is where transport safety policy must begin. This is the important thing, and we should do the important thing first! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! In Europe, Switzerland, Ireland, Spain, Luxembourg, Great Britain, the Federal Republic of Germany, Denmark and Italy still have the 0.8-promille limit. This proves that a majority of those countries, which can be compared with us, have so far adhered to the 0.8-promille limit. They will not claim – and there are also social democratic governments under it – that these governments are acting more irresponsibly to their people than we are. You won't want to put that on anyone! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] The largest and most recent study on this subject is that of Univ.-Prof. Dr. Krüger, University of Würzburg, more than 21,000 drivers in Germany who were subjected to an alcomat sample. It states that only 4 per cent of accidents with alcohol at the tax level occur below 0.8 per milligrams, but 96 per cent of accidents with alcohol at the tax at over 0.8 per milligrams, and that the average alcoholization of the accident tankers was 1.6 per milligrams. These figures, ladies and gentlemen, can essentially also be transferred to Austria, and for this reason it is logical and imperative for the People's Party to begin with those 96 percent of the traffic accidents that occur at more than 0.8 permills. There are three groups, ladies and gentlemen, who are particularly vulnerable, three problem groups: the so-called promille drinkers over 0.8 per mille, the young drivers and drug users. The ÖVP also uses these three main risk groups, which are responsible for most of the accidents on Austria's roads. We have tabled an amendment to the Committee on Transport, as the only party, in order to draw up proposals for solutions specifically for these problem groups. The Social Democratic Party has taken over a large part of the ÖVP proposals with regard to higher penalties. The SPÖ did not want to accept the extension of the pilot licence for young people from two to three years. This must be noted with a crying eye as a prize for the fact that our proposals have found a majority. In future, there will be considerably higher penalties for alcoholic handleers, which will vary according to the degree of alcoholization, because their accident frequency is also much greater, up to four times the previous penalty, up to four months driving licence withdrawal. There is also a mandatory medical examination in the case of higher alcoholisation, there is a need to re-training. Even for those people who are driving a vehicle under the simultaneous drug and alcohol influence, we have greatly aggravated the sanctions. This has succeeded in anchoring new, strict and target group-oriented measures on transport safety policy into the law, and I am glad that we have succeeded. Ladies and gentlemen! On this occasion, I would like to refer once again to the real advantages of our statutory pension insurance system, because these elements are always confirmed by recognized scientists as future-oriented. It is the system of general compulsory insurance, in which a large community of solidarity ensures that the claims of those who are retired are adequately insured and they participate in income development. It is the payment procedure, which has always proved to be excellent compared to other financing systems. It is a system based on the employment relationship in which a corresponding replacement of income is also made in the pension, and it is a system which takes into account that a federal financial contribution is intended as a fixed component. I believe that this flexible system of statutory pensions is the right and appropriate basis for the fact that pensioners – now in retirement and even future pensioners – do not have to worry that they do not have adequate insurance for their claims. We in this country, for example, are trying to compensate for the blatant social policy failure in the world of work, with regard to women, in a quite paradoxical form in the pension system, which, of course, is logically causing the pension system to become partially financially in need, because it is purely labour-related, as the Federal Minister has said as one of the objectives. A purely employment-related pension scheme, which also wants to cover periods in which no acquisition has taken place, must become absolutely necessary on the contribution side. That's where you can do whatever you want. That is why this meticulous commitment to ideas once developed in the past is the first obstacle to real reforms, and I would like to draw your attention to this: As long as you do not go to a mixing system – whether you call it a three-column system, four- and two-and-a-half-column system, that is completely indifferent to me – in which both pay-based components, which in particular have to carry the social guarantee elements, as well as capital-covered elements, more dedicated to maintaining the standard of living in old age, as long as you do not recognize such a mixing system as meaningful and consider how you then implement it so that people can afford it at all, as long as you will not get out of the dead end. And the pension system is in a kind of dead end. This was clearly seen in the reactions to therup study. The rup study would certainly be appropriate to argue now on its basis; one must not always be of the same opinion with what is inside it, but it is at least a relative finding. I am therefore saying "relative" because the study only dealt with a part of social security systems and, for example, did not take into account public service at all – which was not a study assignment; that is already fine – but if one wants to make a truly comprehensive and socially acceptable reform, then one must consider the whole field of over 60, over 65, over I do not know how many years old people have in mind. There can't be another niche policy – I mean the years 2020, 2030 and the following – and above all one has to stop pretending that a reform that was actually due yesterday, which we should at least do today or tomorrow, would have something to do with existing pensions, as if anyone had someday demanded that one intervene in existing pensions. Because, of course, the protection of trust is crucial. Only: Existing pensions can have a dynamic element that looks like this, and that must be available. This is one of the main criticisms I make about this proposal. The idea is right, one must also have a value-reserving element in the system, but any compulsive link with any index – we have learned in the meantime – can go into the eye. This can become inexhaustible, and then it is only quite a promise that cannot be kept. This was, of course, a mistake when one could not afford to do so with the existing pension system, because where should the money come from when the equivalence is cut off at once? This has been done, and now you have to get out of this dilemma, but with an index clause or with a constitutional provision, of course, this is not possible. Young people are willing at any time to offer significant sacrifices for the older generation when they feel and have the security that they pay into a system that is thought out, that is just and that is socially balanced. In front of us is a package of transport safety, which includes many areas of road transport, but which no longer contains some essential points, which we also thought were necessary: These points are the point driver's license, the driving with light in the day and – today and strongly debated – very opposite of course – the reduction of the promille limit from 0.8 to 0.5. This package of road safety regulations provides for a more stringent withdrawal of driving licences at high levels of alcohol from 1.2 per milligrams and the like. It includes the regulation of the installation of wheel clamps to cars of alcoholic handlebars, the advanced steering training of 17 years – subject to certain conditions beginning with 16 according to French model – a time extension of the practical driving licence, 45 minutes for trucks, 25 minutes for cars, a uniform EU driving licence, which will apply in all EU Member States, a theoretical driving licence – the examination is outsourced and computerised – driving licence withdrawal for ghost drivers and so on, and finally a moped driver's license as early as 15 years of age. The latter is particularly important for young people in rural areas to come to their teaching centre, and this will now be possible by means of a regulation by the competent head of state. There are many concerns about it because it is said that the young person is particularly vulnerable to traffic, and the question arises whether he is doing something good with it. The answer will bring the future. But now on the central topic of this afternoon or this evening, the reduction of the blood alcohol content from 0.8 per mille to 0.5 promille. This has not been included in the Government's proposal, but it has been made as a request by the chairman of the Transport Committee, Mr Parnigoni, and his friends by the Social Democrats who make this possible. Why does this request provide for a reduction? We know that alcohol is one of the main causes of accidents in all EU countries and, of course, in addition. Alcohol as the main cause of accidents cannot be denied, can not be disputed away and can not only be explained with high concentrations, because accidents are also present in lower areas. As Austria, as far as road safety is concerned, is in the international con clusion, the reduction of the Promille limit is also a means of a little better cutting off here. Austria, on the other hand, is in the top range in the case of accidents with personal injury. Each 1 000 inhabitants was 5.2 percent in 1994, and that is just too much. And while there are one million inhabitants in the EU's 126 traffic deaths per year, there are 150 in Austria. We're the fifth worst country. Far ahead is Great Britain. On the There are per million inhabitants "only" 64 dead. The EU Road Safety Programme 1997 to 2010 aims to reduce the number of road deaths by 40 percent. So if in 1995 there were 45 000 dead and 1.6 million injured, this figure would almost be halved in the future. The EU Commission's proposal should also be discussed: 0.5 Promille alcohol and driving with light a day. All European studies and practice in Europe confirm the lower risk of accident with 0.5 promille. At 0.8 permill, the accident risk is already four times as high. There is this own-initiative proposal by Mr Parnigoni and Mr Comrades, which is to be debated in a milder form and which is supported by the ARBÖ, if it is said that between 0.8 and 0.5 promille does not take place in the same way as a driver's license, but only in the case of a repetition. There is the example of Sweden. It was introduced there in 1970 and was strictly reviewed there. There are 67 traffic fatalities in Austria 167. I think these are alarming numbers. Furthermore, studies from countries where the alco limit has already been lowered from 0.8 to 0.5 indicate a significant reduction in alcohol driving in all areas, especially in the upper areas above 1.5 per mille. Alcohol-related accidents will show, as well as appropriate judicial convictions, in all levels of alcoholization – and not, as is shown here, only in the highest; also under 0.8 permills – caused and even now already suspected. So there was only one third – listen carefully! – all Alcolenker in the field of 1.5 Promille found in an investigation by the Graz Police Directorate! The ARBÖ also sees the transport safety package as an essential measure to increase road safety. In the future, it will also be decisive whether the self-responsibilities and the self-initiatives of road users can be strengthened through awareness-raising measures. A vehement representative for the reduction to 0.5 Promille is the Austrian Society for Accident Surgery, which, according to its statement, could avoid 50 deaths a year. Infinite suffering, many invalids, family suffering and also high public costs could be dealt with here. Today, Mr Anschober quoted a letter from the Action Community against alcohol at the tax. The personal fate of the grandparents of the family Schauer has led to the creation of this community of action. 60,000 signatures have been spontaneously issued here by the people of Austria to vote for the reduction. That's the triggering moment to say: Something terrible has happened, but not all have to happen. I believe that as a parliamentarian, we are called upon to bring this idea to Parliament as well. From my own experience I can say, as a participant in a drinking experiment organised by the Transport Safety Board a few years ago, that – the then freshly baked transport minister Viktor Klima had invited – already half of the allowed 0.8 Promille no longer has the ability to travel, and not only is proven no longer given by equipment, but also after the purely personal feeling. I believe that this experience must also be taken into account accordingly, and it must be concluded accordingly. If you listen to the population, you know – and you can't ignore that – that there is a clear majority for 0.5 promills. In a survey in Vienna, 80 percent of women and 61 percent of men voted in favour. It is not about, however, saying that there should be only the decision between increased control or 0.5 promills, but it is about simply putting both measures into practice. I invite everyone – even those who are already known in the public to vote for 0.5 – not to vote and remain in their decision, no matter what form. If – which is addressed to the colleagues of the ÖVP – Mrs Landeshuptmann Klasnic in the Styria for 0.5, Niederösterreichs Landeshuptmann Pröll and Minister Bartenstein and Molterer are for 0.5, then it should certainly be possible for their colleagues to join this. I do not have much time, and I would therefore like to make the following request: [[Quoted print version removed]] I ask you to agree. Leave Let me conclude with a sentence from the letter of the Action Community against alcohol at the tax. I quote Marc Aurel: He who does not do anything is often wrong. Whoever does not forbid injustice, if he can, commands it. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e21145	Federal Minister! Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I'm allowed to do it briefly. The liberalisation of the telecommunications sector has achieved two objectives from our point of view. First: The telecommunications infrastructure can now be continuously improved through private capital. Secondly, The larger number of new and innovative providers will launch an idea contest for new products and will also reduce the costs for users. In the U.S., every tenth dollar spent flows into information and communication technology. Japan intends to move over the next few years, with investments of 650 billion D-Mark, a fibre-optic network of 400 000 kilometres in length. According to realistic estimates, liberalisation of telecommunications in Germany will trigger an investment volume of around 50 billion D-marks. The EU Commission expects 1.3 million new jobs in the European telecommunications market by 2005. We must also use this growth impulse for Austria. Here we are required, and this data shows that telecommunications is a market of the future, a growth market of the 21st century. ==References== This applies not only to large enterprises, but also to small and medium-sized enterprises. You can benefit from this development. The Telecommunications Act is also an important step for a modern economic location in Austria. This telecommunications law creates equal market access for new suppliers, ensures fair treatment of remote regions, creates an independent regulator, and therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we create good conditions for the future. However, we should also be aware that, according to recent OECD studies, Austria has the highest telecommunications costs in addition to Ireland and Portugal. That is, an important, perhaps the most important factor for the Austrian site will be cost-effective telecommunications costs. In other words, a reduction in telephone and telecommunications costs in the foreseeable future is necessary, and I hope that the decision-making process on the new telecommunications law will also create the conditions for this.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e21448	Mr President. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Back to the Telecommunications Act. The introduction of the Telecommunications Act in 1993 was a first step towards liberalising the telecommunications market in Austria. The experience gained since this time is reflected in the law to be adopted today. This new Telecommunications Act is primarily about creating fair foundations so that the entire Austrian telecommunications network for postal and competing providers can be used as quickly as possible. The aim of this law is also to establish as flexible a legal framework as possible for the introduction of full competition in the telecommunications market from 1. January 1998. Ladies and gentlemen! In principle, it is regulated. Detailed regulations, which are often very individual, will take place on the basis of the regulation. Furthermore, EU directives in force under the new law are implemented at national level. What opportunities, ladies and gentlemen, do we now offer this new telecommunications law? – It is a basis for economic development that improves Austria’s position in economic competition in a sustainable way. It is the basis for the creation of new and additional jobs that will be highly qualified. It is the basis for ensuring the quality criteria through the presence of high-performance telephone and data transmission lines and corresponding services for users. Conditions are laid down for entrepreneurs to be able to make information exchange efficient and inexpensive. Another important objective must be to ensure access to information, education and business opportunities for disadvantaged regions and groups of people. What does all of this mean? – Telecommunication will be the world’s most growing industry for years to come. The mobile market in Austria is in a strong growth phase. The number of mobile users will rise from 620 000 at the end of 1996 to just under one million by the end of 1997 only as an example. In Austria, around 1.1 million people have access to the worldwide network Internet. This corresponds to 17% of the population aged 14 and over. In fact, however, 650 000 people or 10% of the population use the new medium. – To make just a few numbers aware. Some of the essential provisions of this new law will be: the creation of a Telecom-Control-Ges.m.b.H. regulatory authority, the universal services that take account of a comprehensive supply on the market, the Telekom Control Commission, whose tasks will be defined by law, and, also, an important point, the question of interconnection, which, above all, provides a safeguard clause for small providers. Ladies and gentlemen! In conclusion: This new Telecommunication Act offers a wide range of future prospects for securing, improving and expanding Austria's economic location, improving its supply, providing comprehensive and reliable services for the population and, above all, taking into account the increasing convergence with the media sector. In this sense, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to ask you to approve this draft Government.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e21753	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! High house! As a result of the liberalisation of European air traffic since 1987, many legislative changes to our national law have had to be decided in recent years. In contrast to the US, where there were drastic consequences, the liberalisation steps in European air transport have been gradually and smoothly implemented. In Europe, for example, there were neither dramatic business solutions in the face of major aviation companies nor spectacular price fluctuations. ==References== In the European Economic Area, the third and last liberalisation pacts in force in April 1997 have now changed significantly in Austria's air transport situation. Bilateral air agreements between countries from the European Economic Area have largely lost their importance. [[Abg. Tichy-Schreiter: (where a cell phone is ringing) Who's calling? The Clinton?]] [[Abg. May. Stadler: Oh, là, là, Lord Arbitrator!]] As an example of this new legal situation, only the release of cabotage within the EEA or the administrative simplification by the removal of routes and flight plan permits in EU transport should be mentioned here. In this way, each air carrier in the EEA can connect and fly two points in the European Economic Area. This free access applies, as has been said, but only to EEA airlines. Switzerland, for example, sees this regulation move towards the European end. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! This example shows once again that Austria's accession to the European Economic Area and to the European Union was of fundamental economic importance, even though some groups still do not want to accept it. Furthermore, we have a draft amendment to the Aviation Act, which will bring both a change in the air liability law and an adaptation of parts of the law to the legal situation created by the accession to the European Union. A number of legal changes to our national law will also take place in the course of legal consolidation. These changes have become necessary in order to achieve a balance between competition and control mechanisms. In the case of the reorganisation of the air liability right, it should be stressed that the novelle provides for an unlimited liability to passengers. This will open the way for a number of airlines to take part. This excludes air sports associations in which leisure sports are operated. A liability regulation proposed by the EU Commission, which should enter into force in the near future, also provides for unlimited liability for personal damage under the transport contract. Another focus of the reorganisation of the air liability right is the significant increase in the liability ceiling in the event of third party liability – and at the same time an adaptation to the European standard will be made. For example, in aircraft with a maximum weight of 14 tonnes, the liability is increased from 15 million schilling to 900 million schilling. Ladies and gentlemen, To sum up, I would like to stress that this liberalisation in air transport is worth noting, especially when one knows that since the Second World War aviation has been one of the most highly regulated areas. A short historical trip, which I would like to insert here: At that time, the flight route, tariffs and aircraft type had to be subject to approval by the authorities. . From this point of view, as a Social Democratic, I can agree with the liberalisation of Austrian air traffic with a calm conscience, especially because I believe that these two government documents also take into account the control mechanisms necessary in the tough competition within the aviation industry to protect consumers. Thank you.

High house! Thus, the 1996 higher education report also suggests that the average duration of studies for all graduates over the last 20 years is stable for about 14 semesters despite many changes in higher education and higher education policy. In the 1980s, the number of students who received a scholarship fell steadily. Since the beginning of the 1990s, this number has increased sharply. In the last five years, the number of study beneficiaries has increased by 25 percent, but the number of students has only increased by about 5 percent. Now, children are also streaming a little poorer shifts and workers' children to the universities – a development that has always been a big concern for us social democrats. However, over the last five years, government expenditure in this area has roughly doubled to over 1.2 billion schilling. One goal of this innovation is to make professional activity more attractive during the main holidays and to limit those during the semester. This is to be achieved by a widespread ban on work which exceeds the current limit of 3 740 S per month during the course of the study. The amendment tabled by SPÖ and ÖVP is now also in line with the requirement of Austrian high school students to exclude the employment ban on family support in addition to the main summer holidays. Since the vast majority of the scholarship students also refer to family support and can no longer earn more than 3 740 S outside the holiday, and a study by the Ministry of Science on the social situation of students shows that only one percent of those students who finance their studies to more than 70 percent by a scholarship work regularly, I can accept this proposal. I can also agree with this proposal because it wants to prevent delays in study caused by forced, continuous work. The call for more and more diligent students, who are more and more "shin" – says: However, evidence can become very counterproductive for society in an ever shorter period of time, as this is associated with extreme training. At the end of this development, we may then no longer have universities where students are fully trained and where they also learn to capture social and group dynamics, because universities are threatening to get to last-school higher schools where the "seem" – the testimony – determines consciousness. It is important to prevent such a development. Mr President. High house! There is another problem. The average age of students, although becoming more and more diligent, is increasing. The reason for this lies in a dramatic breakdown of the structures in the student field. The typical student, the typical student who starts studying after school and completes with the middle of 20 of this study, is getting more and more rare, her number is not even half of the students. The number of those students who work before or during study or pursue another activity is increasing steadily. In fact, we have three groups at the universities today: first, the group of classical students with a degree degree in about the middle of 20, second, the group of those who are currently employed or previously in an employment relationship, and third, the group of senior students. This change has not yet been achieved, although it is in the interests of the current Federal Government that, in order to be more flexible, more than one professional field is being learned in a professional life. We Socialists and Democrats have now initiated a working group dealing with the working students, examining their social situation more closely and proposing better study opportunities. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dkfm. Holger Bauer]] Already more than 30 percent of all students work regularly. As many of them obtain a scholarship, no one knows, most likely it is a very small part. The age structure of the scholarships is also completely unknown. A study must also be possible in addition to the profession. There are still a lot of courses in which no evening courses can be held and examination applications can in principle only be made at office hours or only in the morning. In these areas of facilitation and perhaps to provide support for the starting phase, similar to the help for workers in the last semester of the evening school, is my concern. An Austrian parliamentary delegation was there last year and looked at it. This nuclear milestone is on an earthquake line, but this Parliamentary Commission was assured last year that everything was fine and that Slovenia was considering an exit from nuclear policy. This is quite different today. In the meantime, an international committee of experts has found that there are seventy most serious defects in this age-weak nuclear milestone and that at the beginning of this year this nuclear power plant automatically shut down, of which the public was not informed at all or too late. This really moves me, and I am therefore very glad that there has been a five-party proposal aimed at the exit of the Eastern countries from nuclear policy. However, the wording chosen here is much too soft for me personally, because it should mean: A ratification of Austria's accession to an Eastern European country is only possible if it declares binding the exit from nuclear policy. ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-09-020-XX-NRSITZ-00081_d7e25075	Mr President. Federal Minister! The law of police co-operation in the decision-making process has two priorities. It regulates, on the one hand, the exchange of information with foreign security authorities and security organisations, on the other, the intervention of the Austrian security authorities abroad and foreign authorities in Austria. With the process of European integration, international cooperation between security authorities is becoming increasingly important. While the political changes in Europe have brought substantial relief to many citizens, such as greater freedom of travel, they also offer new opportunities and markets for criminals. This international crime, of course, is trying to strengthen its efforts to take a foothold in the rich states of Europe, thus presenting a serious danger and threat to the stability of our society. This organised crime can only be effectively combated if there is cross-border cooperation with other countries. Drug trafficking, trafficking in human beings and innumerable economic crimes are increasingly controlled by organised crime. The huge profits are washed in or used for corruption. In the course of numerous criminal proceedings several hundred million shillings have been frozen in recent years. In order to combat these machinations of organised crime across national borders, very intensive international cooperation is needed. Austria can bring a great deal of experience into international police cooperation in this area. The current police cooperation law will further increase the impact of security authorities in the fight against international crime. Police cooperation means that the information is centrally collected and evaluated so that the security authorities can react early and efficiently. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Stadler.]] Because only with a uniform legal practice and an internationally uniform procedure can we combat this organized crime worldwide. The Police Cooperation Act ensures Europe-wide cooperation. It is also a sufficient basis for a modern and successful work of the executive and also an effective means of curbing international organised crime. That is why we will also agree to this law. As far as the present motion for a resolution by Mr Blünegger and Mr Comrades is concerned, there is no agreement on this, as all the measures which ensure and ensure comprehensive border surveillance and control have already been put in place by the Federal Minister for the Interior.
